\displaystyle\int d\Phi_{\rm rad,2}^{\,(abcd)}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{(4\pi^{2})^{\epsilon-4}}{\pi\,\Gamma^{2}(1/2-\epsilon)}\,{(s_{abcd})}^{2-2\epsilon}\,\!\!\int_{0}^{1}\!\!\!dx^{\prime}\,\big[x^{\prime}(1-x^{\prime})\big]^{-1/2-\epsilon}\int_{0}^{1}\!\!\!dy^{\prime}\!\int_{0}^{1}\!\!\!dz^{\prime}\!\int_{0}^{\pi}\!\!\!d\phi\,\sin^{-2\epsilon}\phi\;\times

(5.48)

\displaystyle\times\int_{0}^{1}\!\!\!dy\!\int_{0}^{1}\!\!\!dz\,\Big[y^{\prime}(1-y^{\prime})^{2}\,z^{\prime}(1-z^{\prime})\,y^{2}(1-y)^{2}\,z(1-z)\Big]^{-\epsilon}(1-y^{\prime})\,y(1-y)\,,

where
y^{\prime}
and
z^{\prime}
are the variables relative to the
secondary-radiation phase space, and
x^{\prime}
parametrises the azimuth between
subsequent emissions.

As an example, we consider the contribution to the double soft barred limit
\overline{\bf S}_{ij}\,RR
stemming from the
q\bar{q}
configuration. The core structure of such a limit embeds the NNLO soft current in Eq.(96) of Ref.
[
102
]
, which reads

\displaystyle\mathcal{I}_{cd}^{(ij)}=\frac{s_{ic}\,s_{jd}+s_{id}\,s_{jc}-s_{ij}\,s_{cd}}{s_{ij}^{2}\,(s_{ic}+s_{jc})\,(s_{id}+s_{jd})}\;,

(5.49)

with
i,j
referring to the unresolved partons, and
c,d
to the emitting particles. To integrate this current we choose to parametrise the double-unresolved phase space according to the
(ijcd)
mapping.
In this parametrisation, the denominators appearing in Eq.(
5.49
) read

\displaystyle s_{ij}=y^{\prime}y\,\bar{s}_{cd}^{(ijcd)},\quad s_{ic}+s_{jc}=(1-y^{\prime})y\,\bar{s}_{cd}^{(ijcd)}\,,\quad s_{id}+s_{jd}=(y^{\prime}+z-y^{\prime}z)(1-y)\,\bar{s}_{cd}^{(ijcd)},

(5.50)

while in the numerators only polynomials in the Catani-Seymour parameters appear. Note that also the dependence on the azimuth is completely factorised and occurs only in the numerator through
s_{jd}
and
s_{id}
, since

\displaystyle s_{jd}=(1-y)\left[y^{\prime}z^{\prime}(1-z)+(1-z^{\prime})z+2(1-2x^{\prime})\sqrt{y^{\prime}z^{\prime}(1-z^{\prime})z(1-z)}\right]\bar{s}_{cd}^{(ijcd)}\,,

(5.51)

and
s_{id}=(y^{\prime}+z-y^{\prime}z)(1-y)\,\bar{s}_{cd}^{(ijcd)}-s_{jd}
.
The phase space integral assumes the following form

\displaystyle\int d\Phi_{\rm rad,2}^{\,(abcd)}\,\mathcal{I}_{cd}^{(ij)}

\displaystyle\propto

\displaystyle{(\bar{s}_{cd}^{(ijcd)})}^{-2\epsilon}\,\!\!\int_{0}^{1}\!\!\!dx^{\prime}\,\big[x^{\prime}(1-x^{\prime})\big]^{-1/2-\epsilon}\int_{0}^{1}\!\!\!dy^{\prime}\!\int_{0}^{1}\!\!\!dz^{\prime}\!\int_{0}^{\pi}\!\!\!d\phi\,\sin^{-2\epsilon}\phi\;\times

(5.52)

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{1}\!\!\!dy\!\int_{0}^{1}\!\!\!dz\,\Big[y^{\prime}(1-y^{\prime})^{2}\,z^{\prime}(1-z^{\prime})\,y^{2}(1-y)^{2}\,z(1-z)\Big]^{-\epsilon}\frac{\mathcal{N}}{(y^{\prime})^{2}\;y^{2}\;(y^{\prime}+z-y^{\prime}z)}\;,

where the numerator reads in full generality

\displaystyle\mathcal{N}=z^{\ell_{1}}(1-z)^{\ell_{2}}\;y^{m_{1}}(1-y)^{m_{2}}\;(z^{\prime})^{n_{1}}(1-z^{\prime})^{n_{2}}\;(y^{\prime})^{r_{1}}(1-y^{\prime})^{r_{2}}(1-2x^{\prime})^{k}\;.

(5.53)

Now we sketch the integration procedure by considering one variable at a time: the integration over
\phi
is trivial, the one over
y
returns a simple Beta function
B(m_{1}-1-2\epsilon,m_{2}+1-2\epsilon)
, with
m_{1},m_{2}\in\mathbb{Z}
, and the azimuth contribution is
B(1/2-\epsilon,1/2-\epsilon)\,\delta_{k\,0}
. The trivial dependence on
z^{\prime}
in the numerator enables a straightforward integration that returns
B(n_{1}+1-\epsilon,n_{2}+1-\epsilon)
, with
n_{1},n_{2}
being integers or semi-integers. The
z
variable features instead a less-trivial structure, which however can be integrated according to

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{1}dz\;\frac{z^{\ell_{1}-\epsilon}\,(1-z)^{\ell_{2}-\epsilon}}{y^{\prime}+z-y^{\prime}\,z}=B(\ell_{1}+1-\epsilon,\ell_{2}+1-\epsilon)\,{}_{2}F_{1}(1,\ell_{2}+1-\epsilon,\ell_{1}+\ell_{2}+2-2\epsilon,1-y^{\prime})\;.

(5.54)

The remaining integration over
y^{\prime}
is tackled by applying recursively the hypergeometric function properties until we obtain
{}_{2}F_{1}(-\epsilon,-2\epsilon,1-2\epsilon,1-y^{\prime})
. The series expansion of such class of hypergeometric functions is known at all orders in
\epsilon
in terms of Spence functions.
At this point the poles in
\epsilon
can be extracted using the
plus
prescription and the remaining integration over
y
can be carried out with standard techniques.

We stress that the
q\bar{q}
case is particularly simple, since no denominators containing the azimuth appear in the current structure after the parametrisation.
For the gg case (and for the collinear contributions) the integration is much more involved.
However, in our approach it can be carried out with standard techniques
[
104
]
.
Similar integrals have been computed in the context of other NNLO schemes,
for instance by means of integration by parts identities
and differential equations machinery
[
109
,
110
]
.

5.5
Double virtual contribution

Given a general strategy to define double-unresolved and real-virtual counterterms (see Sec
5.4
) we have to identify the IR singularities of the double-virtual matrix element, that we assume to be already UV renormalised. From the studies carried on in the context of IR factorisation
[
111
,
112
,
113
]
, the infrared poles of gauge theory scattering amplitudes are known to organise according to

\displaystyle\mathcal{A}\left(\frac{p_{i}}{\mu},\alpha_{s}(\mu),\epsilon\right)=\mathbf{Z}\left(\frac{p_{i}}{\mu},\alpha_{s}(\mu),\epsilon\right)\mathcal{H}\left(\frac{p_{i}}{\mu},\alpha_{s}(\mu),\epsilon\right)\,,

(5.55)

where
\mathcal{A}
is a generic
n
-parton amplitude,
\mathcal{H}
is finite for
\epsilon\rightarrow 0
and
\mathbf{Z}
is a color operator with a universal form. In Eq.(
5.55
) all the color indices are understood, to simplify the notation. The operator
\mathbf{Z}
obeys a renormalisation group equation that can be solved in terms of the anomalous dimension
\mathbf{\Gamma}
as described by the following expression

\displaystyle\mathbf{Z}\left(\frac{p_{i}}{\mu},\alpha_{s}(\mu),\epsilon\right)=\mathcal{P}\exp\left\{\int_{0}^{\mu}\frac{d\lambda}{\lambda}\;\mathbf{\Gamma}\left(\frac{p_{i}}{\lambda},\alpha_{s}(\lambda),\epsilon\right)\right\}\;.

(5.56)

The operator
\mathbf{\Gamma}
, in turn, manifests a universal behaviour regulated by the
dipole formula

[
114
,
113
,
111
,
112
]

\displaystyle\mathbf{\Gamma}\left(\frac{p_{i}}{\lambda},\alpha_{s}(\lambda),\epsilon\right)=\frac{1}{2}\,\widehat{\gamma}_{k}(\alpha_{s}(\lambda,\epsilon))\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}i,j>i=1\end{subarray}}^{n}\ln\left(\frac{2\,p_{i}\cdot p_{j}\,\ e^{i\pi\sigma_{ij}}}{\lambda^{2}}\right)\mathbf{T}_{i}\cdot\mathbf{T}_{j}-\sum_{i=1}^{n}\gamma_{i}(\alpha_{s}(\lambda,\epsilon))\;,

(5.57)

where the
\sigma_{ij}
is a phase factors that equals 1 if
i,j
are both in the initial or in the final state, and vanishes otherwise. The function
\widehat{\gamma}_{k}
is a universal quantity related to the cusp anomalous dimension, and the jet anomalous dimensions
\gamma_{i}
are related to the anomalous dimensions of quark and gluon fields. Finally,
\mathbf{T}_{a}
are color operators
[
115
,
58
]
. By expanding
\mathbf{\Gamma}
at two-loop order, and then deriving the expression of
\mathbf{Z}
, it is straightforward to obtain the singular part of the squared amplitude up to
\alpha^{2}_{s}
by means of Eq.(
5.55
).
As a result, the double virtual matrix element features infrared poles that obey the following general structure
[
112
]
[
116
]
:

\displaystyle VV_{\rm poles}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\Big(\frac{\alpha_{s}}{\pi}\Big)^{2}\bigg[-\frac{1}{8\epsilon^{4}}\Big(\sum_{i}C_{f_{i}}\Big)^{2}B+\frac{1}{4\epsilon^{3}}\Big(\sum_{i}C_{f_{i}}\Big)\Big(\frac{3}{8}b_{0}+2\sum_{j}\gamma_{j}^{(1)}\Big)B+\frac{1}{4\,\epsilon^{2}}\bigg[\Big(-\frac{b_{0}}{2}\sum_{i}\gamma_{i}^{(1)}

\displaystyle-

\displaystyle\frac{\widehat{\gamma}_{k}^{(2)}}{4}\sum_{i}C_{f_{i}}-2\Big(\sum_{i}\gamma_{i}^{(1)}\Big)^{2}\Big)B+\frac{b_{0}}{4}\sum_{i,j\neq i}\ln\frac{s_{ij}}{\mu^{2}}\,B_{ij}+\frac{1}{4}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}i,j\neq i\\
k,l\neq k\end{subarray}}\ln\frac{s_{ij}}{\mu^{2}}\,\ln\frac{s_{kl}}{\mu^{2}}\,B_{ijkl}\bigg]

\displaystyle+

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2\epsilon}\bigg[\sum_{i}\gamma_{i}^{(2)}B-\frac{\widehat{\gamma}_{k}^{(2)}}{4}\sum_{i,j\neq i}\ln\frac{s_{ij}}{\mu^{2}}\,B_{ij}-\sum_{i,j\neq i}\ln\frac{s_{ij}}{\mu^{2}}H_{ij}\bigg]\bigg]-\frac{\alpha_{s}}{\pi}\bigg[\frac{1}{2\epsilon^{2}}\sum_{i}C_{f_{i}}-\frac{1}{\epsilon}\sum_{i}\gamma_{i}^{(1)}\bigg]V

with
C_{f_{i}}
being the Casimir eigenvalue for the leg
i
and
b_{0}=(11C_{A}-4T_{R}N_{f})/3
being the one loop
\beta
-function coefficient. The
quantity
H_{ij}
is a process-dependent finite contribution that derives from the virtual matrix element, while
B_{ab}
and
B_{abcd}
are respectively the single and double colour-correlated Born matrix elements:

\displaystyle B_{ab}\equiv\langle\mathcal{A}_{B}|\mathbf{T}_{a}\cdot\mathbf{T}_{b}|\mathcal{A}_{B}\rangle\,,\qquad B_{abcd}\equiv\langle\mathcal{A}_{B}|\{\mathbf{T}_{a}\cdot\mathbf{T}_{b}\,,\,\mathbf{T}_{c}\cdot\mathbf{T}_{d}\}|\mathcal{A}_{B}\rangle\,.

(5.59)

From Eq.(
) it is evident that such a structure can be implemented in the subtraction procedure only given the knowledge of the necessary anomalous dimensions, which however can be found in the literature.
Moreover, also the process-dependent quantity
H_{ij}
has to be considered as an external input of the scheme.

5.6
Application:
T_{R}C_{F}
contribution to
e^{+}e^{-}\rightarrow jj
at NNLO

The validation of the subtraction scheme has been performed for the two jet production in
e^{+}e^{-}
annihilation, considering for the moment only the
T_{R}C_{F}
contribution
[
91
]
.
The virtual
e^{+}e^{-}\rightarrow q_{1}\bar{q}_{2}
, real-virtual
e^{+}e^{-}\rightarrow q_{1}\bar{q}_{2}g_{[34]}
, and double real
e^{+}e^{-}\rightarrow q_{1}\bar{q}_{2}q^{\prime}_{3}\bar{q}^{\prime}_{4}
contributions to the inclusive cross-section are known analytically from Ref.
[
21
,
117
,
118
]

\displaystyle\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!VV=B\Big(\frac{\alpha_{s}}{2\pi}\Big)^{2}T_{R}C_{F}\bigg\{\Big(\frac{\mu^{2}}{s}\Big)^{2\epsilon}\Big[\frac{1}{3\epsilon^{3}}+\frac{14}{9\epsilon^{2}}+\frac{1}{\epsilon}\Big(\frac{353}{54}-\frac{11}{18}\pi^{2}\Big)+\frac{7541}{324}-\frac{77}{27}\pi^{2}-\frac{26}{9}\zeta_{3}\Big]

(5.60)

\displaystyle\hskip 99.58464pt+\Big(\frac{\mu^{2}}{s}\Big)^{\epsilon}\Big[-\frac{4}{3\epsilon^{3}}-\frac{2}{\epsilon^{2}}+\frac{1}{\epsilon}\Big(-\frac{16}{3}+\frac{7}{9}\pi^{2}\Big)-\frac{32}{3}+\frac{7}{6}\pi^{2}+\frac{28}{9}\zeta_{3}\Big]\bigg\}

\displaystyle\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\int d\Phi_{\rm rad}\,RV=B\Big(\frac{\alpha_{s}}{2\pi}\Big)^{2}T_{R}C_{F}\Big(\frac{\mu^{2}}{s}\Big)^{\epsilon}\Big[\frac{4}{3\epsilon^{3}}+\frac{2}{\epsilon^{2}}+\frac{1}{\epsilon}\Big(\frac{19}{3}-\frac{7}{9}\pi^{2}\Big)+\frac{109}{6}-\frac{7}{6}\pi^{2}-\frac{100}{9}\zeta_{3}\Big]

\displaystyle\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\int d\Phi_{\rm rad,2}\,RR=-B\Big(\frac{\alpha_{s}}{2\pi}\Big)^{2}T_{R}C_{F}\Big(\frac{\mu^{2}}{s}\Big)^{2\epsilon}\Big[\frac{1}{3\epsilon^{3}}+\frac{14}{9\epsilon^{2}}+\frac{1}{\epsilon}\Big(\frac{407}{54}-\frac{11}{18}\pi^{2}\Big)+\frac{11753}{324}-\frac{77}{27}\pi^{2}-\frac{134}{9}\zeta_{3}\Big]\;.

We can now compute the local counterterms and their integrated counterparts, showing the cancellation of the singularities presented above.
The double real matrix element presents single phase space singularities corresponding to the single collinear limit only.
The double-unresolved singularities arise from the configurations where both the emitted quarks are soft, or they are collinear to one of the hard Born-level fermion. The relevant limits in the unbarred kinematics
are
\overline{\bf C}_{ij}RR,\,\overline{\bf S}_{ij}RR,\,\overline{\bf C}_{ijk}RR,\,\overline{\bf S}_{ij}\overline{\bf C}_{ijk}RR
, on top of the NLO limits, relevant for the real-virtual counterterm.
Here
\{i,j\}=\{3,4\}
, and
\{ijk\}=\{134,234\}
, and
r=\{1,2,3,4\},r\neq i,j,k
. The resulting complete set of counterterms is then given by

\displaystyle\overline{K}^{(1)}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\overline{\bf C}_{34}RR\;,

(5.61)

\displaystyle\overline{K}^{(2)}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\Big(\overline{\bf S}_{34}+\overline{\bf C}_{123}(1-\overline{\bf S}_{34})+\overline{\bf C}_{234}(1-\overline{\bf S}_{34})\Big)RR\;,

(5.62)

\displaystyle\overline{K}^{(12)}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\overline{\bf C}_{34}\Big(\overline{\bf S}_{34}+\overline{\bf C}_{123}(1-\overline{\bf S}_{34})+\overline{\bf C}_{234}(1-\overline{\bf S}_{34})\Big)RR\;,

(5.63)

\displaystyle\overline{K}^{(RV)}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{\alpha_{s}}{2\pi}\frac{2}{3\epsilon}T_{R}\Big[\overline{\bf S}_{[34]}+\overline{\bf C}_{1[34]}\big(1-\overline{\bf S}_{[34]}\big)+\overline{\bf C}_{2[34]}\big(1-\overline{\bf S}_{[34]}\big)\Big]\,R\;.

(5.64)

The explicit definitions of the contributing limits in the remapped kinematic are reported in Ref.
[
91
]
.
In the evaluation of the corresponding integral we need to introduce an appropriate mapping, and then apply the integration strategy sketched in the previous sections. In particular

\displaystyle\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\int\!d\Phi_{\rm rad,2}\,\overline{\bf S}_{ij}\,RR=\mathcal{N}_{1}^{\,2}\,T_{R}C_{F}\sum_{c,d=1}^{2}B_{cd}\Big(\{\bar{k}\}^{(ijcd)}\Big)\int d\Phi_{\rm rad,2}^{(ijcd)}\;\times

\displaystyle\hskip 85.35826pt\times\Big[\frac{s_{ic}\,s_{jd}+s_{id}\,s_{jc}-s_{ij}\,s_{cd}}{s_{ij}^{2}\,(s_{ic}+s_{jc})\,(s_{id}+s_{jd})}-\frac{s_{ic}\,s_{jc}+s_{ic}\,s_{jc}}{s_{ij}^{2}\,(s_{ic}+s_{jc})^{2}}-\frac{s_{id}\,s_{jd}+s_{id}\,s_{jd}}{s_{ij}^{2}\,(s_{id}+s_{jd})^{2}}\Big]

\displaystyle\hskip 62.59605pt=-B\Big(\frac{\alpha_{s}}{2\pi}\Big)^{2}T_{R}C_{F}\Big(\frac{\mu^{2}}{s}\Big)^{2\epsilon}\Big[\frac{1}{3\epsilon^{3}}+\frac{17}{9\epsilon^{2}}+\frac{1}{\epsilon}\Big(\frac{232}{27}-\frac{7}{18}\pi^{2}\Big)+\frac{2948}{81}-\frac{131}{54}\pi^{2}-\frac{38}{9}\zeta_{3}\Big],

\displaystyle\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\int\!d\Phi_{\rm rad,2}\,\overline{\bf C}_{ijk}\,RR=\frac{\mathcal{N}_{1}^{\,2}}{2}\,B_{\mu\nu}\Big(\{\bar{k}\}^{(ijkr)}\Big)\int d\Phi_{\rm rad,2}^{(ijkr)}\;\frac{P_{ijk}^{\mu\nu}}{s_{ijk}}

\displaystyle\hskip 66.86397pt=-B\Big(\frac{\alpha_{s}}{2\pi}\Big)^{2}T_{R}C_{F}\Big(\frac{\mu^{2}}{s}\Big)^{2\epsilon}\Big[\frac{1}{3\epsilon^{3}}+\frac{31}{18\epsilon^{2}}+\frac{1}{\epsilon}\Big(\frac{889}{108}-\frac{1}{2}\pi^{2}\Big)+\frac{23941}{648}-\frac{31}{12}\pi^{2}-\frac{80}{9}\zeta_{3}\Big].

Let us stress that the spin-dependent component of the double-collinear Altarelli-Parisi splitting function vanishes upon integration. Finally, the composite limit
{\bf S}_{ij}{\bf C}_{ijk}RR
coincides with the double soft contribution
{\bf S}_{ij}RR
, given the fact that
k
and
r
have to be different from
i,j
, and in this specific process they can only coincide with 1 and 2. Summing all the contributions, as prescribed by Eq.(
5.62
), we easily obtain the double-unresolved integrated counterterm

\displaystyle I^{(2)}=B\Big(\frac{\alpha_{s}}{2\pi}\Big)^{2}T_{R}C_{F}\Big(\frac{\mu^{2}}{s}\Big)^{2\epsilon}\Big[-\frac{1}{3\epsilon^{3}}-\frac{14}{9\epsilon^{2}}+\frac{1}{\epsilon}\Big(\frac{11}{18}\pi^{2}-\frac{425}{54}\Big)+\frac{12149}{324}+\frac{74}{27}\pi^{2}+\frac{122}{9}\zeta_{3}\Big]\;.

(5.65)

The next contribution is due to the single unresolved configurations, which are entirely reproduced by the collinear limit
\overline{\bf C}_{34}
. The expression of
T_{R}C_{F}
contribution to
I^{(1)}
can be directly read from Eq.(
5.44
) returning

\displaystyle I_{hq}^{(1)}=-\frac{\alpha_{s}}{2\pi}\Big(\frac{\mu^{2}}{s}\Big)^{\epsilon}\frac{2}{3}T_{R}\Big(\frac{1}{\epsilon}-\log\bar{\eta}_{[34]r}+\frac{8}{3}\Big)R\,\overline{\mathcal{W}}_{hq}

(5.66)

here
\{h,q\}=\{1,2,[34]\}
.
The mixed-double unresolved counterterm is given by

\displaystyle I^{(12)}_{hq}=-\frac{\alpha_{s}}{2\pi}\frac{2}{3}T_{R}\Big(\frac{\mu^{2}}{s}\Big)^{\epsilon}\Big(\frac{1}{\epsilon}-\log\bar{\eta}_{[34]r}+\frac{8}{3}\Big)\Big[\overline{\bf S}_{h}+\overline{\bf C}_{hq}(1-\overline{\bf S}_{h})\Big]R\,\overline{\mathcal{W}}_{hq}

(5.67)

Finally, the real-virtual counterterm reads

\displaystyle\overline{K}_{hq}^{(\rm RV)}=\frac{\alpha_{s}}{2\pi}\,\frac{2}{3}T_{R}\frac{1}{\epsilon}\Big[\overline{\bf S}_{h}+\overline{\bf C}_{hq}(1-\overline{\bf S}_{h})\Big]R\,\overline{\mathcal{W}}_{hq}\;,

(5.68)

and its integrated counterpart that results

\displaystyle I^{(RV)}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{\alpha_{s}}{2\pi}\frac{2}{3}\frac{1}{\epsilon}T_{R}\int d\Phi_{\rm rad}\Big[\overline{\bf S}_{[34]}+\overline{\bf C}_{1[34]}(1-\overline{\bf S}_{[34]})+\overline{\bf C}_{2[34]}(1-\overline{\bf S}_{[34]})\Big]R

(5.69)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle B\Big(\frac{\alpha_{s}}{2\pi}\Big)^{2}T_{R}C_{F}\Big(\frac{\mu^{2}}{s}\Big)^{\epsilon}\Big[\frac{4}{3\epsilon^{3}}+\frac{2}{\epsilon^{2}}+\frac{1}{\epsilon}\Big(\frac{20}{3}-\frac{7}{9}\pi^{2}\Big)+20-\frac{7}{6}\pi^{2}+\frac{100}{9}\zeta_{3}\Big]

We can check verify that all the expected cancellations take place.
The subtracted double real matrix element is finite by construction, the difference
\overline{K}^{(RV)}+I^{(12)}
has to be finite sector-by-sector, and indeed we have

\displaystyle\overline{K}_{hq}^{(RV)}+I^{(12)}_{hq}=-\frac{\alpha_{s}}{2\pi}\,\frac{2}{3}T_{R}\Big(\log\frac{\mu^{2}}{s_{[34]r}}+\frac{8}{3}\Big)\Big[\overline{\bf S}_{h}+\overline{\bf C}_{hq}(1-\overline{\bf S}_{h})\Big]R\,\overline{\mathcal{W}}_{hq}\;,

(5.70)

which is clearly free of explicit poles. The real-virtual matrix element has to be finite for
\epsilon\rightarrow 0
when combined with
I^{(1)}
, thanks to the KLN theorem. Indeed we have

\displaystyle RV\,\overline{\mathcal{W}}_{hq}+I^{(1)}_{hq}=-\frac{\alpha_{s}}{2\pi}\,\frac{2}{3}T_{R}\Big(\log\frac{\mu^{2}}{s_{[34]r}}+\frac{8}{3}\Big)\,R\,\overline{\mathcal{W}}_{hq}\;.

(5.71)

The comparison between Eq.(
5.70
) and Eq.(
5.71
) makes evident that the phase space singularities of the two objects cancel in the combination
RV\,\overline{\mathcal{W}}_{hq}+I_{hq}-(\overline{K}_{hq}^{(\rm RV)}+I^{(12)}_{hq})
. Finally, the double-virtual poles are cancelled by the sum
I^{(2)}+I^{(\rm RV)}
, as one can easily deduce by looking at the expressions in Eqs.(
5.65
)-(
5.69
).

5.7
Discussion

In this report we have reviewed the main aspects of the
local analytic sector subtraction scheme.

We have recently computed all the integrated counterterms that are necessary to have a fully general subtraction method (for massless, final-state QCD).

It is evident from the discussion above that the scheme benefits from an optimised partition and parametrisation of the phase space, which allows for the analytic integration of the singular kernels arising both from the double real and the real-virtual matrix element.

Currently, the subtraction scheme is fully validated at NLO for a generic process with final state partons.
With regards to NNLO, we have computed the
C_{F}T_{R}
contribution to the inclusive
e^{+}e^{-}\rightarrow jj
cross-section.
Much work is in progress to further check the scheme in less trivial processes, towards its the generalisation to any final state QCD process.

Beyond this, the next steps will concern the extension of the scheme to the treatment of initial state radiation. Very recently, a preliminary successful study has been carried out at NLO. Extending it to NNLO
is expected to be time-consuming, but not to present conceptual novelties. However we foresee to be able to propose a similar structure as the one for final-state radiation. The generalisation to the massive case is expected to be more involved, especially concerning the integration procedure.

6
The
q_{\scriptscriptstyle\rm T}
-subtraction method

In the recent years a huge effort was made by the experimental community to
increase the accuracy of high-energy physics measurements. On the theory
side, then, it has become mandatory to aim at a deeper understanding of the
perturbative behaviour of the Standard Model (SM), which translates into a
need for better control on higher-order calculations, as long as on the
issues they yield.

One of them addresses the handling of IR divergences appearing in
the intermediate stages of higher-order QCD computations. Once their cancellation is
under control at numerical level, order-by-order in the relevant coupling
constant, a step forward is made towards an attempt of a tentative SM
falsification, within the comparison with the experimental result.

Monte Carlo generators regularise the IR divergences appearing in real and
virtual contributions to scattering amplitudes with subtraction
prescriptions. Such subtraction methods are not only capable of producing
total cross sections as well as differential distributions, but they also
allow the implementation of the same selection cuts imposed by the
experiment.

In order to expose the cancellation of the IR divergences between real and
virtual contributions, the behaviour of the scattering amplitudes at the
boundaries of the phase space is the key ingredient used by subtraction
methods, such as the well-established ones proposed in
Refs.
[
58
,
59
]
for NLO computations and those
developed for NNLO calculations. Examples of them are the
transverse-momentum (
q_{\scriptscriptstyle\rm T}
) subtraction method
[
119
,
120
,
121
]
, the
N
-jettiness
subtraction
[
122
,
123
]
, the
projection-to-Born
[
97
]
, the residue
subtraction
[
124
,
99
]
and the antenna
subtraction method
[
94
,
95
,
125
]
,
15
15

15

An elaborated discussion of the antenna subtraction method shall be
presented in Section
7
.

which have all been successfully applied to LHC
phenomenology. The
q_{\scriptscriptstyle\rm T}
-subtraction method was also applied for the first
time to differential cross sections (for hadron-hadron collisions) at N
3
LO
in Ref.
[
126
]
. Other N
3
LO differential computations can be
found in Refs.
[
127
,
128
,
129
,
130
]
. Also, N
3
LO differential results to jet production in deep
inelastic scattering (DIS) and charged current DIS were calculated using the
projection-to-Born method in Ref.
[
131
]

and
[
132
]
, respectively.
Moreover, the
q_{\scriptscriptstyle\rm T}
-subtraction method was also extended in order to deal
with massive partons in the final state
[
133
,
134
]
, with initial-state QED corrections
[
135
]
,
with final-state QED radiation
[
136
]
and recently with
mixed QCD–QED corrections at full NNLO
[
137
]
.

6.1
The master formula of the
q_{\scriptscriptstyle\rm T}
-subtraction method

We consider the inclusive hard-scattering reaction

h_{1}(p_{1})+h_{2}(p_{2})\to F(\{q_{i}\})+X\,,

(6.1)

where
h_{1}
and
h_{2}
are two hadrons colliding with momenta
p_{1}
and
p_{2}

and triggering the final-state system
F
along with an arbitrary and
undetected final state
X
.
The observed final state
F
consists of a system of non-QCD partons composed
by one or more colour-singlet particles, e.g. vector bosons, photons, Higgs
bosons, Drell–Yan lepton pairs, etc., with four-momenta
q_{i}
. The total
four-momentum of the system
F
is denoted by
q
, with

q=\sum_{i}q_{i}\,,

(6.2)

and can be expressed in terms of the total invariant mass
M
,
the transverse momentum
{\bf q_{\scriptscriptstyle\rm T}}
w.r.t. the direction of the
colliding hadrons, and the rapidity

y=\frac{1}{2}\,\log\frac{p_{2}\cdot q}{p_{1}\cdot q}

(6.3)

in the centre-of-mass system of the collision. Since
F
is colourless,
the LO partonic Born cross section can be either initiated by
qq^{\prime}

annihilation, as in the case of the Drell–Yan process, or by gluon–gluon
fusion, as in the case of Higgs boson production.

In order to describe the structure of the subtraction formalism we first notice
that, at LO, the transverse momentum

{\bf q_{\scriptscriptstyle\rm T}}=\sum_{i}{\bf q_{\scriptscriptstyle\rm T}}_{i}

(6.4)

of the final state system
F
is identically zero. Therefore, as long as
q_{\scriptscriptstyle\rm T}\neq 0
, the N
n
LO QCD contributions, with
n=1,2,3
, are given by the
N
n-1
LO QCD contributions to the triggered final state

F
+jet(s).
16
16

16

The notation N
n
LO stands for: N
0
LO=LO,
N
1
LO=NLO, N
2
LO=NNLO, and so forth.
Consequently, if
q_{\scriptscriptstyle\rm T}\neq 0

we have

\mathrm{d}\sigma^{\scriptscriptstyle F}_{\scriptscriptstyle\rm N^{n}LO}(q_{\scriptscriptstyle\rm T}\neq 0)\equiv\mathrm{d}\sigma^{\scriptscriptstyle F+{\rm jets}}_{\rm N^{\scriptscriptstyle n-1}LO}\,,\qquad n=1,2,3\,.

(6.5)

implying that, if
q_{\scriptscriptstyle\rm T}\neq 0
, the IR divergences
appearing in the computation of
\mathrm{d}\sigma^{\scriptscriptstyle F}_{\scriptscriptstyle\rm N^{n}LO}(q_{\scriptscriptstyle\rm T}\neq 0)
are those already present in
\mathrm{d}\sigma^{\scriptscriptstyle F+{\rm jets}}_{\scriptscriptstyle\rm N^{n-1}LO}
.

The IR singularities involved in
\mathrm{d}\sigma^{\scriptscriptstyle F}_{\scriptscriptstyle\rm N^{n}LO}(q_{\scriptscriptstyle\rm T}\neq 0)
can be handled and cancelled by the available subtraction methods at
N
n-1
LO. The only remaining singularities at N
n
LO are associated with
the limit
q_{\scriptscriptstyle\rm T}\to 0
and are treated with the
q_{\scriptscriptstyle\rm T}
-subtraction method.
17
17

17

This point is a great advantage for
q_{\scriptscriptstyle\rm T}
-subtraction, since the method profits from lower-order results. However, this also alters the specific IR behaviour of the contributions, preventing a fully local cancellation.

Since the small-
q_{\scriptscriptstyle\rm T}
behaviour of the transverse-momentum cross section is well
known through the resummation program of logarithmically-enhanced
contributions to transverse-momentum distributions, see
Refs.
[
138
,
139
,
140
,
141
,
142
,
143
,
144
,
145
,
146
,
147
]
, we exploit this knowledge
to build the necessary counterterms in order to subtract the remaining
singularity, thus promoting the
q_{\scriptscriptstyle\rm T}
-subtraction method proposed in
Refs.
[
119
,
120
,
148
]
to N
3
LO.

The sketchy form of the
q_{\scriptscriptstyle\rm T}
-subtraction method for the N
n
LO cross
section, see Ref.
[
119
]
, is

\mathrm{d}\sigma^{\scriptscriptstyle F}_{\scriptscriptstyle\rm N^{n}LO}={\cal H}^{\scriptscriptstyle F}_{\scriptscriptstyle\rm N^{n}LO}\otimes\mathrm{d}\sigma^{\scriptscriptstyle F}_{\rm LO}+\left[\mathrm{d}\sigma^{\scriptscriptstyle F+{\rm jets}}_{\scriptscriptstyle\rm N^{n-1}LO}-\mathrm{d}\sigma^{\scriptscriptstyle F\;{\rm CT}}_{\scriptscriptstyle\rm N^{n}LO}\right],\qquad n=1,2,3\,,

(6.6)

where
\mathrm{d}\sigma^{\scriptscriptstyle F\;{\rm CT}}_{\scriptscriptstyle\rm N^{n}LO}
is the contribution
of the counterterm to the N
n
LO cross section which cancels the divergences
of
\mathrm{d}\sigma^{\scriptscriptstyle F+{\rm jets}}_{\rm N^{n-1}LO}
in the limit
q_{\scriptscriptstyle\rm T}\to 0
. The
n
-order counterterm can be written as

\mathrm{d}\sigma^{\scriptscriptstyle F\;{\rm CT}}_{\scriptscriptstyle\rm N^{n}LO}=\Sigma^{\scriptscriptstyle F}_{\rm N^{n}LO}\left(\frac{q_{\scriptscriptstyle\rm T}^{2}}{M^{2}}\right)\mathrm{d}^{2}{\bf q_{\scriptscriptstyle\rm T}}\otimes\mathrm{d}\sigma^{\scriptscriptstyle F}_{\scriptscriptstyle\rm LO}\;,

(6.7)

where the symbol
\otimes
stands for convolutions over momentum fractions
and sum over flavour indices of the partons. More precisely, the function

\Sigma^{\scriptscriptstyle F}_{\scriptscriptstyle\rm N^{n}LO}(q_{\scriptscriptstyle\rm T}^{2}/M^{2})
is the
n
-order truncation of
the perturbative series in
\alpha_{s}

\Sigma^{\scriptscriptstyle F}_{c\bar{c}\leftarrow a_{1}a_{2}}\!\left(\frac{q_{\scriptscriptstyle\rm T}^{2}}{M^{2}}\right)=\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\frac{\alpha_{s}}{\pi}^{n}\Sigma^{\scriptscriptstyle F;(n)}_{c\bar{c}\leftarrow a_{1}a_{2}}\!\left(\frac{q_{\scriptscriptstyle\rm T}^{2}}{M^{2}}\right),

(6.8)

where the labels
a_{1}
and
a_{2}
stands for the partonic channels of the
N
n
LO correction to the Born cross section (
\mathrm{d}\sigma^{\scriptscriptstyle F}_{\rm LO}\equiv\mathrm{d}\left[\sigma^{\scriptscriptstyle F;(0)}_{c\bar{c}}\right]
). Notice that at LO the
only available configuration is
a_{1}=c
and
a_{2}=\bar{c}
, where

c\bar{c}
is (are) the partonic channel(s) at which the LO cross section is
initiated. The function
\Sigma^{\scriptscriptstyle F}(q_{\scriptscriptstyle\rm T}^{2}/M^{2})
embodies all the
logarithmic terms that are divergent in the limit
q_{\scriptscriptstyle\rm T}\to 0
, reproducing the
singular behaviour of
\mathrm{d}\sigma^{\scriptscriptstyle F+{\rm jets}}
in the small-
q_{\scriptscriptstyle\rm T}

limit.
18
18

18

These counterterms have a universal structure, and are local in the variable
q_{T}
.

The counterterm is defined free of terms proportional to

\delta(q_{\scriptscriptstyle\rm T}^{2})
, which are all considered in the perturbative factor
{\cal H}^{\scriptscriptstyle F}
. The hard coefficient function
{\cal H}^{\scriptscriptstyle F}_{\scriptscriptstyle\rm N^{n}LO}
, that encodes all the IR finite terms of the
n
-loop
contributions, is obtained by the N
n
LO truncation of the perturbative
function

{\cal H}^{\scriptscriptstyle F}_{c\bar{c}\leftarrow a_{1}a_{2}}(z;\alpha_{s})=\delta_{c\,a_{1}}\,\delta_{\bar{c}\,a_{2}}\;\delta(1-z)+\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\left(\frac{\alpha_{s}}{\pi}\right)^{n}{\cal H}^{\scriptscriptstyle F;(n)}_{c\bar{c}\leftarrow a_{1}a_{2}}(z)\;\;,

(6.9)

where
z=M^{2}/s
.
19
19

19

The definition of the hard coefficients requires the computation of
the virtual matrix elements in
d
-dimensions, in order to explicitly remove the poles. Thus, the real-virtual cancellation of singularities is not fully local, as in
fdu
.

According to the transverse momentum resummation formula,
see Eq. (10) of Ref.
[
120
]
, and using the Fourier
transformation between the conjugate variables
{\bf q_{\scriptscriptstyle\rm T}}
and
b
(the impact
parameter), the perturbative hard function
{\cal H}^{\scriptscriptstyle F}
and the
counterterm are obtained by the fixed order truncation of the following
identity

\displaystyle\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\left[\Sigma^{\scriptscriptstyle F}_{c\bar{c}\leftarrow a_{1}a_{2}}\left(\frac{q_{\scriptscriptstyle\rm T}^{2}}{M^{2}}\right)+{\cal H}^{\scriptscriptstyle F}_{c\bar{c}\leftarrow a_{1}a_{2}}\left(\frac{M^{2}}{s};\,\alpha_{s}\right)\right]\otimes\mathrm{d}\left[\hat{\sigma}^{\scriptscriptstyle F;(0)}_{c\bar{c}}\right]=

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{M^{2}}{s}\,\int_{0}^{\infty}\mathrm{d}b\,\frac{b}{2}\,J_{0}(b\,{q_{\scriptscriptstyle\rm T}})\,S_{c}(M,b)

\displaystyle\times\int_{x_{1}}^{1}\frac{\mathrm{d}z_{1}}{z_{1}}\,\int_{x_{2}}^{1}\frac{\mathrm{d}z_{2}}{z_{2}}\,\mathrm{d}\hat{\sigma}^{\scriptscriptstyle F;(0)}_{c\bar{c}}\,f_{a_{1}/h_{1}}\!\left(\frac{x_{1}}{z_{1}},\,\frac{b_{0}^{2}}{b^{2}}\right)f_{a_{2}/h_{2}}\!\left(\frac{x_{2}}{z_{2}},\,\frac{b_{0}^{2}}{b^{2}}\right)\otimes\left[H^{\scriptscriptstyle F}\,C_{1}\,C_{2}\right]_{c\bar{c};a_{1}a_{2}}\,,

where
J_{0}(b\,{\bf q_{\scriptscriptstyle\rm T}})
is the 0th-order Bessel function,
f_{c/h}

corresponds to the distribution of a parton
c
in a hadron
h
and
b_{0}=2e^{-\gamma_{\scriptscriptstyle\rm E}}
(
\gamma_{\scriptscriptstyle\rm E}=0.5772...
is the Euler number). The symbolic
factor
\mathrm{d}\hat{\sigma}^{\scriptscriptstyle F;(0)}_{c\bar{c}}
for the partonic Born
cross section
\hat{\sigma}^{\scriptscriptstyle F;(0)}_{c\bar{c}}
denotes

\mathrm{d}\hat{\sigma}^{\scriptscriptstyle F;(0)}_{c\bar{c}}\equiv\frac{\mathrm{d}\hat{\sigma}^{\scriptscriptstyle F;(0)}_{c\bar{c}}}{\mathrm{d}\phi}\,,

(6.11)

where
\phi
represents the phase space of the final-state system
F
.
In the l.h.s. of Eq. (
6.1
) the convolution (as well as the sum
over the flavour indices of the partons) between the resummation functions

\Sigma^{\scriptscriptstyle F}_{c\bar{c}}
and
{\cal H}^{\scriptscriptstyle F}_{c\bar{c}}
, the partonic Born
cross section and the parton distributions is symbolically denoted by

\otimes\,\mathrm{d}\left[\hat{\sigma}^{\scriptscriptstyle F;(0)}_{c\bar{c}}\right]
.

The large logarithmic corrections are exponentiated in the Sudakov form
factor
S_{c}(M,b)
of the quark (
c=q,{\bar{q}}
) or of the gluon (
c=g
),
that has the following expression

S_{c}(M,b)=\exp\left\{-\int_{b_{0}^{2}/b^{2}}^{M^{2}}\frac{\mathrm{d}q^{2}}{q^{2}}\left[A_{c}\left(\alpha_{s}\left(q^{2}\right)\right)\,\log\frac{M^{2}}{q^{2}}+B_{c}\left(\alpha_{s}\left(q^{2}\right)\right)\right]\right\}.

(6.12)

The functions
A
and
B
in Eq. (
6.12
) are perturbative series in

\alpha_{s}
:

\displaystyle A_{c}(\alpha_{s})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\left(\frac{\alpha_{s}}{\pi}\right)^{n}A_{c}^{(n)}\,,\,

(6.13)

\displaystyle B_{c}(\alpha_{s})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\left(\frac{\alpha_{s}}{\pi}\right)^{n}B_{c}^{(n)}\,.

(6.14)

The structure of the symbolic factor denoted by
\left[H^{\scriptscriptstyle F=H}\,C_{1}\,C_{2}\right]_{c\bar{c};a_{1}a_{2}}
, that strongly depends on the initial-state
channel of the Born subprocess, is explained with detail in
Refs.
[
149
,
121
]
.

6.2
Higher-order power corrections at NLO

There are subtraction methods which are independent of any regularising
parameter and proceed by building local counterterms and point-wisely
subtracting the IR divergences along the phase space – thus they are
mentioned to be
local
, while other, such as the
q_{\scriptscriptstyle\rm T}
-subtraction
method, introduce a regularising, or
slicing
, parameter, i.e. a cutoff
scale, in order to separate different IR regions.
20
20

20

The main advantage of
q_{\scriptscriptstyle\rm T}
-subtraction is its universality for achieving a cancellation of singularities, which allow to apply the method to several processes up to NNLO. The local structure of the required counterterms is much more complicated than the one obtained within this formalism.

Such separation of the phase space introduces instabilities in the numerical
evaluation of cross sections and differential
distributions
[
150
,
151
,
152
,
153
]
, and some care has to be taken in order to obtain stable
and reliable results. Furthermore, the knowledge of logarithmic and
power-correction terms in the cutoff plays a relevant role in the
identification of universal structures, in the development of regularisation
prescriptions and in resummation programs
[
138
,
139
,
140
,
141
,
142
,
143
,
144
,
145
,
146
,
147
,
154
]
.

In Ref.
[
155
]
a study was conducted about how power
corrections in the cutoff may affect the application of the
q_{\scriptscriptstyle\rm T}
-subtraction
method to the production of a colourless system at next-to-leading order in
the strong coupling constant
\alpha_{s}
– in particular, to Drell–Yan vector (V)
and Higgs (H) boson production in gluon fusion at NLO in QCD, in the
infinite top-mass limit.

In fact, the singular terms in the small-cutoff limit are universal and are
cancelled by the application of the
q_{\scriptscriptstyle\rm T}
-subtraction (or other methods),
while finite and vanishing terms are, in general, process dependent and thus,
after the subtraction procedure, a residual dependence on the cutoff remains
as power corrections. While these terms formally vanish in the null cutoff
limit, they give a non-zero numerical contribution for any finite choice of
the cutoff.

If one is able to take into account such terms, not only our understanding of
the perturbative behaviour of QCD cross sections increases from a theoretical
point of view, but also the numerical implementation of the subtraction
becomes more robust, since the power terms weaken the dependence of the final
result on the arbitrary cutoff. Notice that this becomes more relevant from a
numerical point of view, when applied to higher-order calculation, as pointed
out, for example, in the evaluation of NNLO cross sections in
Refs.
[
152
,
153
]
.

Power corrections at NLO have been extensively studied in refs.
[
156
,
157
,
158
,
159
,
160
,
161
,
162
,
163
,
164
,
165
]
both for
N
-jettiness and transverse momentum distributions, in the context of the
N
-jettiness subtraction method, and in refs.
[
166
,
167
,
168
,
169
,
170
,
171
]
within SCET-based subtraction methods. Power corrections at NLO for the transverse momentum of a colour singlet have been derived for the first time at differential level in ref.
[
172
]
within the SCET framework. This study has been followed, with a different method, by ref.
[
155
]
, which is considered in more detail in the following, and by ref.
[
136
]
, which among other new results was able to confirm the former. A numerical extraction of power corrections in the context of NNLL’+NNLO calculations was done in
N
-jettiness
[
154
]
, and a general discussion in the context of the fixed-order implementation of the
N
-jettiness subtraction can be found in ref.
[
123
]
.

6.3
Power corrections for V and H production at NLO in QCD

In Ref.
[
155
]
it is considered the production of a colourless
system
F
of squared invariant mass
Q^{2}
plus a coloured system
X
at a
hadron collider

h_{1}+h_{2}\to F+X\,.

(6.15)

The hadronic cross section can be written as

\displaystyle\sigma

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{a,b}\int_{\tau}^{1}\mathrm{d}x_{1}\int_{\frac{\tau}{x_{1}}}^{1}\mathrm{d}x_{2}\,f_{a}\!\left(x_{1}\right)f_{b}\!\left(x_{2}\right)\int\mathrm{d}q_{\scriptscriptstyle\rm T}^{2}\,\mathrm{d}z\,\frac{\mathrm{d}\hat{\sigma}_{ab}\!\left(q_{\scriptscriptstyle\rm T},z\right)}{dq_{\scriptscriptstyle\rm T}^{2}}\,\delta\!\left(\!z-\frac{Q^{2}}{s}\right),

(6.16)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{a,b}\tau\int_{\tau}^{1}\frac{\mathrm{d}z}{z}\,{\cal L}_{ab}\!\left(\frac{\tau}{z}\right)\frac{1}{z}\int\!\mathrm{d}q_{\scriptscriptstyle\rm T}^{2}\,\frac{\mathrm{d}\hat{\sigma}_{ab}\!\left(q_{\scriptscriptstyle\rm T},z\right)}{dq_{\scriptscriptstyle\rm T}^{2}}\,,

where

\tau=\frac{Q^{2}}{S}\,,\qquad z=\frac{Q^{2}}{s}\,,

(6.17)

f_{a/b}
are the parton densities of the partons
a
and
b
, in the hadron

h_{1}
and
h_{2}
respectively,
S
is the hadronic squared centre-of-mass
energy,
s
is the partonic squared centre-of-mass energy, equal to

s=S\,x_{1}\,x_{2}\,,

(6.18)

\mathrm{d}\hat{\sigma}_{ab}
is the partonic cross section for the process
a+b\to F+X
,
q_{\scriptscriptstyle\rm T}
is the transverse momentum of the system
F
with respect to the
hadronic beams and the luminosity function is defined as

{\cal L}_{ab}(y)\equiv\int_{y}^{1}\frac{\mathrm{d}x}{x}\,f_{a}\!\left(x\right)f_{b}\!\left(\frac{y}{x}\right).

(6.19)

The dependence on the renormalisation and factorisation scales and on the
other kinematic invariants of the process are implicitly assumed.

In the small-
q_{\scriptscriptstyle\rm T}
region, i.e.
q_{\scriptscriptstyle\rm T}\ll Q
, the real contribution to the
perturbative partonic cross sections appearing in Eq. (
6.16
)
contains well-known logarithmically-enhanced terms that are singular in the

q_{\scriptscriptstyle\rm T}\to 0
limit
[
138
,
139
,
140
,
141
,
142
,
143
,
144
,
145
,
146
,
147
]
.
The general structure of the power-correction terms, at variance with that of
the singular logarithms, is unknown. Thus, it is useful to inquire about it,
in order to find out whether it can as well be derived a universal structure,
or whether at least part of it follows a universal behaviour in connection
with its infrared limit.

On the other hand, in order to actually extract the power corrections, the
starting point is the real contribution at small
q_{\scriptscriptstyle\rm T}
to the processes,
first at parton level:

\hat{\sigma}_{ab}^{\scriptscriptstyle<}(z)\equiv\int_{0}^{\left(q_{\scriptscriptstyle\rm T}^{\scriptscriptstyle\rm cut}\right)^{2}}\!\!\mathrm{d}q_{\scriptscriptstyle\rm T}^{2}\,\frac{\mathrm{d}\hat{\sigma}_{ab}(q_{\scriptscriptstyle\rm T},z)}{\mathrm{d}q_{\scriptscriptstyle\rm T}^{2}}\,.

(6.20)

Since in this case the total cross section is analytically known, one may
refer to the above-
q_{\scriptscriptstyle\rm T}^{\scriptscriptstyle\rm cut}
region

\hat{\sigma}_{ab}^{\scriptscriptstyle>}(z)=\int_{\left(q_{\scriptscriptstyle\rm T}^{\scriptscriptstyle\rm cut}\right)^{2}}^{\left(q_{\scriptscriptstyle\rm T}^{\scriptscriptstyle\rm max}\right)^{2}}\!\!\mathrm{d}q_{\scriptscriptstyle\rm T}^{2}\,\frac{\mathrm{d}\hat{\sigma}_{ab}(q_{\scriptscriptstyle\rm T},z)}{\mathrm{d}q_{\scriptscriptstyle\rm T}^{2}}\,,

(6.21)

where
q_{\scriptscriptstyle\rm T}^{\scriptscriptstyle\rm max}
is the maximum value for
q_{\scriptscriptstyle\rm T}
allowed by the kinematics, and
derive the the below-
q_{\scriptscriptstyle\rm T}^{\scriptscriptstyle\rm cut}
contribution as a difference.

At hadron level, when a cut on the transverse momentum is imposed, the
reality of the parton-level cross sections restricts the
z
-integration

\displaystyle\sigma_{ab}^{\scriptscriptstyle<}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\tau\int_{\tau}^{1-f(a)}\frac{\mathrm{d}z}{z}\,{\cal L}_{ab}\!\left(\frac{\tau}{z}\right)\frac{1}{z}\,\hat{\sigma}_{ab}^{\scriptscriptstyle<}(z)

(6.22)

\displaystyle\equiv

\displaystyle\tau\int_{\tau}^{1}\frac{\mathrm{d}z}{z}\,{\cal L}_{ab}\!\left(\frac{\tau}{z}\right)\hat{\sigma}^{\scriptscriptstyle(0)}\,\hat{R}_{ab}(z)\,,

where

a=\frac{q_{\scriptscriptstyle\rm T}^{2}}{Q^{2}}

(6.23)

is the chosen basis for presenting the power corrections and

\hat{\sigma}^{\scriptscriptstyle(0)}
is the partonic Born-level cross section for the
production of the colourless system
F
.

In the second line of Eq. (
6.22
) the
z
-integration limit is
extended to one, aiming to make contact with the transverse-momentum
subtraction formulae, that describe the behaviour of the cross sections in
the soft and collinear limits, namely a Born-like kinematics is required. The
function
\hat{R}_{ab}(z)
is defined within this purpose and admits a
perturbative expansion in
\alpha_{s}
, whose coefficient functions
\hat{R}_{ab}^{\scriptscriptstyle(n)}(z)
can be computed as power series in
a
. Here the
interest is driven to the NLO coefficient, whose form is well-known in
literature
[
173
]
up to the vanishing power-correction terms

\hat{R}_{ab}^{\scriptscriptstyle(1)}(z)=\log^{2}(a)\,\hat{R}_{ab}^{\scriptscriptstyle(1,2,0)}(z)+\log(a)\,\hat{R}_{ab}^{\scriptscriptstyle(1,1,0)}(z)+\hat{R}_{ab}^{\scriptscriptstyle(1,0,0)}(z)+{\cal O}\!\left(a^{\frac{1}{2}}\log{a}\right).

(6.24)

Notice that in Refs.
[
173
,
120
]
, the following
associations hold

\displaystyle R_{ab}^{(1,2,0)}(z)\quad

\displaystyle\leftrightarrow

\displaystyle\quad\Sigma_{c\bar{c}\leftarrow ab}^{F(1;2)}(z)

(6.25)

\displaystyle R_{ab}^{(1,1,0)}(z)\quad

\displaystyle\leftrightarrow

\displaystyle\quad\Sigma_{c\bar{c}\leftarrow ab}^{F(1;1)}(z)

(6.26)

\displaystyle{\cal H}_{c\bar{c}\leftarrow ab}^{F(1)}(z)\quad

\displaystyle\leftrightarrow

\displaystyle\quad R_{ab}^{(1,0,0)}(z)\,.

(6.27)

The aim of Ref.
[
155
]
is the computation of the missing orders
in Eq. (
6.24
). In order to achieve such result, in a way similar to
what is showed in Eq. (
6.21
),
21
21

21

The same method was used in
Refs.
[
173
,
174
]
, at leading power in
a
, to
extract the soft constant of the
q_{\scriptscriptstyle\rm T}
-subtraction hard function and the
second-order collinear coefficient functions for the
q_{\scriptscriptstyle\rm T}
-resummation.

the function
\hat{G}_{ab}(z)
is introduced via the definition

\sigma_{ab}^{\scriptscriptstyle>}=\tau\int_{\tau}^{1-f(a)}\frac{\mathrm{d}z}{z}\,{\cal L}_{ab}\!\left(\frac{\tau}{z}\right)\frac{1}{z}\,\hat{\sigma}_{ab}^{\scriptscriptstyle>}(z)\equiv\tau\int_{\tau}^{1}\frac{\mathrm{d}z}{z}\,{\cal L}_{ab}\!\left(\frac{\tau}{z}\right)\hat{\sigma}^{\scriptscriptstyle(0)}\,\hat{G}_{ab}(z)\,.

(6.28)

At first order in
\alpha_{s}
it holds

\sigma_{ab}^{\scriptscriptstyle>(1)}=\tau\int_{\tau}^{1-f(a)}\frac{\mathrm{d}z}{z}\,{\cal L}_{ab}\!\left(\frac{\tau}{z}\right)\frac{1}{z}\,\hat{\sigma}_{ab}^{\scriptscriptstyle>(1)}(z)=\tau\int_{\tau}^{1}\frac{\mathrm{d}z}{z}\,{\cal L}_{ab}\!\left(\frac{\tau}{z}\right)\hat{\sigma}^{\scriptscriptstyle(0)}\,\hat{G}_{ab}^{\scriptscriptstyle(1)}(z)\,.

(6.29)

and a process-independent formula is elaborated in the paper in order to
transform an integral of the form of the first one in Eq. (
6.29
)
into the form of the second one, producing the series expansion of

\hat{G}_{ab}^{\scriptscriptstyle(1)}(z)
in
a
. Also, the procedure enables to reach any order
in the transverse momentum cut-off.

The results are lengthy and the reader is referred to the original
paper. Here, it will suffice to remember that, for the calculation of these
functions, all the terms originating from the manipulation of the
contributions proportional to the Altarelli–Parisi splitting functions at
the level of the partonic cross sections constitute the so-called “universal
part” of the results.

The general form of the
\hat{G}_{ab}^{\scriptscriptstyle(1)}(z)
functions reads

\displaystyle\hat{G}_{ab}^{\scriptscriptstyle(1)}(z)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle{}\log^{2}(a)\,\hat{G}_{ab}^{\scriptscriptstyle(1,2,0)}(z)+\log(a)\,\hat{G}_{ab}^{\scriptscriptstyle(1,1,0)}(z)+\hat{G}_{ab}^{\scriptscriptstyle(1,0,0)}(z)

(6.30)

\displaystyle{}+a\log(a)\,\hat{G}_{ab}^{\scriptscriptstyle(1,1,2)}(z)+a\,\hat{G}_{ab}^{\scriptscriptstyle(1,0,2)}(z)

\displaystyle{}+a^{2}\log(a)\,\hat{G}_{ab}^{\scriptscriptstyle(1,1,4)}(z)+a^{2}\,\hat{G}_{ab}^{\scriptscriptstyle(1,0,4)}(z)+{\cal O}\!\left(a^{\frac{5}{2}}\log(a)\right),

all the other coefficients being zero. The terms in the first line of
Eq. (
6.30
) are referred to as as leading terms (LT). These
terms are either logarithmically divergent or finite in the
a\to 0
limit.
The terms in the sum in the second line of Eq. (
6.30
) are
referred to as next-to-leading terms (NLT), the first two terms in the
third line as next-to-next-to-leading terms (N
2
LT), and so forth.

The results display some important features:

i)

no odd-power corrections of
\sqrt{a}=q_{\scriptscriptstyle\rm T}^{\scriptscriptstyle\rm cut}/Q
appear in the NLT
and N
2
LT terms;

ii)

the NLT and N
2
LT terms are at most linearly dependent on

\log(a)
;

iii)

the non-universal contribution in fact appears to be highly dependent
on the process at stake, thus making impossible a generalisation of the
procedure to any process.

6.4
Discussion

Although there is not a general proof, what is found for the inclusive cross
section expanded up to
(q_{\scriptscriptstyle\rm T}^{\scriptscriptstyle\rm cut})^{4}
, i.e. the absence of odd-power
corrections in
q_{\scriptscriptstyle\rm T}^{\scriptscriptstyle\rm cut}
, is thought to be valid even at higher orders. One is
not to expect this to be true, in general, for more exclusive quantities –
to this regard, see also Ref.
[
165
]
.

Aside from this, it is useful to remark the importance of the knowledge of
power-corrections terms within the
q_{\scriptscriptstyle\rm T}
-subtraction method.

In the original paper
[
119
]
, the expansion in
\alpha_{s}
of the
transverse-momentum resummation formula generates exactly the three terms in
Eq. (
6.24
), plus extra power-correction terms. In the formula for

\hat{R}_{ab}^{\scriptscriptstyle(1)}(z)
that one can build from the new expression of

\hat{G}_{ab}^{\scriptscriptstyle(1)}(z)
, by changing the overall sign and adding the

\delta(1-z)
contribution from the virtual correction, the power-correction
terms are exactly those produced by the expansion of the real amplitudes.
If one is interested in using the formula for
\hat{R}_{ab}^{\scriptscriptstyle(1)}(z)
to
reduce the dependence on the transverse-momentum cutoff, within the

{q_{\scriptscriptstyle\rm T}}
-subtraction method, the aforementioned extra terms need then to be
subtracted from our expression of
\hat{R}_{ab}^{\scriptscriptstyle(1)}(z)
.

On the other hand, the knowledge of power terms is also crucial for
understanding both the non-trivial behaviour of cross sections at the
boundaries of the phase space, and the resummation structure at subleading
orders. At the same time, within the
q_{\scriptscriptstyle\rm T}
-subtraction method, the knowledge
of the power terms helps in reducing the cutoff dependence of the cross
sections.

While the application of the
q_{\scriptscriptstyle\rm T}
-subtraction method in NLO calculations is
superseded by well-known local subtraction methods, at NNLO it still plays a
major role, also in view of the fact that, as shown in
Refs.
[
152
,
126
]
, the sensitivity to the
numerical value of the cutoff increases at higher orders. This also explains
why it is of interest the calculation of the power corrections to an NNLO
cross section.

7
Antenna subtraction scheme

In this section, we present the antenna subtraction scheme for perturbative QCD calculations. This method has been derived
in
[
94
]
and successfully applied to the calculation of the NNLO corrections to 3-jet production and related event shape observables in electron-positron annihilation
in
[
175
]
. The extension of the scheme to the treatment of initial state radiation relevant for calculations of jet observables in hadronic collisions at LHC has been
established at NLO in
[
95
]
and at NNLO
in
[
176
,
177
,
178
,
179
,
180
,
181
,
182
,
125
]
.
In a first subsection we review the NLO version of the scheme followed by the generalisation to NNLO. In the last subsection we provide our conclusions on the status of the
method and its current implementation for precision phenomenological studies at the LHC.

7.1
Antenna subtraction at NLO

To specify the notation, we define the LO contribution to an
m
-jet
cross section by,

{\rm d}\sigma_{LO}=\int_{{\rm d}\Phi_{m}}{\rm d}\sigma^{B}\;J_{m}^{(m)}(\{p_{m}\})

(7.1)

where the partonic cross section
{\rm d}\sigma^{B}
is related to the square of the tree level amplitude of the process,
integrated over the appropriate
m
-particle phase space
{\rm d}\Phi_{m}
, subject to the kinematical constraint that precisely
m
-jets are observed. The latter constraint is imposed
by the jet function
J_{m}^{(m)}(\{p_{m}\})
, that at this order selects
m
-jets from
m
-final state particles within the four-momentum set
\{p_{m}\}
using an IR safe jet-algorithm.

At NLO, we consider the following
m
-jet cross section,

{\rm d}\sigma_{NLO}=\int_{{\rm d}\Phi_{m}}{\rm d}\sigma^{V}\;J_{m}^{(m)}(\{p_{m}\})+\int_{{\rm d}\Phi_{m+1}}{\rm d}\sigma^{R}\;J_{m}^{(m+1)}(\{p_{m+1}\})

(7.2)

where
{\rm d}\sigma^{V}
is the UV-renormalised one loop virtual correction to the
m
-parton Born cross section
{\rm d}\sigma^{B}
, and
{\rm d}\sigma^{R}
is the tree-level
squared amplitude for a single real radiation emission from the Born process.

Although the sum in Eq. (
7.2
) is finite in
d=4
dimensions, each of the two integrals is separately divergent if
d=4
. Using dimensional regularisation with
space-time dimension equal to
4-2\epsilon
, the divergences (arising from the integration over the loop-momentum in
{\rm d}\sigma^{V}
) appear as
explicit

double
1/\epsilon^{2}
and single
1/\epsilon
poles. On the other hand, the real correction
{\rm d}\sigma^{R}
being finite in
d=4
, has singularities when it is integrated over
the phase space regions corresponding to soft and collinear emission which are allowed by the jet function
J_{m}^{(m+1)}(\{p_{m+1}\})
, which selects
m
-jets from an
(m+1)
particle
phase space. It is precisely the contribution of unresolved emission to the
m
-jet cross section from the real correction that generates the
implicit
IR singularities in this contribution.

Given that the individual contributions in Eq. (
7.2
) live in phase spaces of different dimensionality and in particular, both contribute to the evaluation of an arbitrary
observable, which often requires the imposition of arbitrary sets of experimental cuts on the phase space integration, it is necessary that the IR singularities must be cancelled
prior to any numerical calculation.

The antenna subtraction method is a subtraction procedure which allows for the isolation of the infrared singularities present in intermediate steps of higher-oder perturbative QCD calculations.
The procedure consists in
adding and subtracting a
countertem
that reproduces the singular behaviour of the real correction, that is simple enough
that it can be integrated analytically in the single-radiative phase space
and combined with the virtual contribution. The NLO cross section becomes,

\displaystyle{\rm d}\sigma_{NLO}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int_{{\rm d}\Phi_{m}}\bigg({\rm d}\sigma^{V}\;J_{m}^{(m)}(\{p_{m}\})+\int_{1}{\rm d}\sigma^{S}\;J_{m}^{(m)}(\{p_{m})\}\bigg)

(7.3)

\displaystyle+

\displaystyle\int_{{\rm d}\Phi_{m+1}}\bigg({\rm d}\sigma^{R}\;J_{m}^{(m+1)}(\{p_{m+1}\})-{\rm d}\sigma^{S}\;J_{m}^{(m)}(\{\tilde{p}_{m}\})\bigg)\,.

The contribution
{\rm d}\sigma^{S}
in Eq. (
7.3
) is a counterterm which reproduces the same singular divergent behaviour as the real emission matrix element

{\rm d}\sigma^{R}
in all appropriate limits. In particular, for an IR-safe observable in a singular soft or collinear phase space region, the following conditions are satisfied,

\displaystyle J_{m}^{(m+1)}(\{p_{m+1}\})

\displaystyle\to

\displaystyle J_{m}^{(m)}(\{p_{m}\})

\displaystyle J_{m}^{(m)}(\{\tilde{p}_{m}\})

\displaystyle\to

\displaystyle J_{m}^{(m)}(\{p_{m}\})

\displaystyle{\rm d}\sigma^{S}

\displaystyle\to

\displaystyle{\rm d}\sigma^{R}\;,

(7.4)

such that the bottom line in Eq. (
7.3
) can be integrated numerically in four dimensions.

We note that the first condition in Eq. (
7.4
) is satisfied automatically for all IR-safe observables,
while the remaining conditions are enforced by the subtraction scheme. In particular, any QCD amplitude with the emission of one unresolved parton in
{\rm d}\sigma^{R}
can be written as a product
of the Born amplitude times a soft and collinear factor which contains all the singular terms. As it will be shown below, the antenna subtraction counterterms will have the same
factorised structure and employ a remapping of the real emission phase
\{p_{m+1}\}\to\{\tilde{p}_{m}\}
space that preserves the on-shellness and momentum conservation in
the underlying Born configuration in the counterterm contribution. This guarantees that the remaining conditions in (
7.4
) are satisfied. Finally, the counterterm contribution
{\rm d}\sigma^{S}

has to be integrated analytically over all singular regions of the 1-parton radiative subspace, leading to explicit
1/\epsilon
poles that can be combined with the virtual contribution in Eq. (
7.3
),
thus cancelling all the divergences and allowing the remaining numerical integration over the
m
-parton phase space in the first line of Eq. (
7.3
) to be performed in
d
=4 dimensions.

A key characteristic in the antenna subtraction scheme is the subtraction of the infrared singularities following the singularity structure in colour-ordered amplitudes. In a given colour basis, QCD amplitudes
decompose into leading and subleading colour contributions with the singularities in colour-ordered amplitudes only occurring between colour adjacent partons. In this way, a NLO real emission squared tree-level
colour ordered amplitude factorises as,

\displaystyle|{\cal M}^{0}_{m+1}(1,\ldots,i,j,k,\ldots,m+1)|^{2}

\displaystyle\mathrel{{\mathop{\longrightarrow}\limits}}

\displaystyle S_{ijk}\;|{\cal M}_{m}^{0}(1,\ldots,i,k,\ldots,m+1)|^{2},

(7.5)

when gluon
j
is soft between colour adjacent partons
i
and
k
, with the singular eikonal factor given by,

\displaystyle S_{ijk}=\frac{2s_{ik}}{s_{ij}s_{jk}}\quad\textrm{with}\quad s_{ij}=(p_{i}+p_{j})^{2}\;.

(7.6)

Similarly in the limit where a quark and gluon pair become collinear, the colour-ordered amplitudes factorise. If quark
i
and gluon
j
become collinear and form quark
k
, then the colour adjacent
i,j
pair gives
a singular contribution,

\displaystyle|{\cal M}^{0}_{m+1}(1,\ldots,i,j,\ldots,m+1)|^{2}

\displaystyle\mathrel{{\mathop{\longrightarrow}\limits}}

\displaystyle\frac{1}{s_{ij}}P_{qg\to q}(z)|{\cal M}^{0}_{m}(1,\ldots,k,\ldots,m+1)|^{2}

(7.7)

while a separated quark/gluon pair does not,

\displaystyle|{\cal M}^{0}_{m+1}(1,\ldots,i,\ldots,j,\ldots,m+1)|^{2}

\displaystyle\mathrel{{\mathop{\longrightarrow}\limits}}

\displaystyle\textrm{finite}.

(7.8)

In Eq. (
7.7
),
z
is the fraction of momentum carried by one of the collinear partons and the collinear splitting function
P_{qg\to q}
is given by,

\displaystyle P_{qg\to q}(z)=\left(\frac{1+(1-z)^{2}-\epsilon z^{2}}{z}\right).

(7.9)

At NLO with one unresolved emission, the only kinematical configurations that generate IR singular contributions in the real emission tree-level squared amplitudes
in
{\rm d}\sigma^{R}
are the configurations corresponding to a single soft or single collinear emission. Looking at Eqs. (
7.5
) and (
7.7
) we observe that in these limits,
the real emission amplitudes obey a factorisation formula in terms of universal singular factors multiplied by a born-like reduced matrix element. The basic idea of the antenna subtraction
approach is to derive the subtraction terms with antenna functions which encapsulate all singular limits due to the emission of unresolved partons between two colour-connected hard partons.
The full antenna subtraction term is then obtained by summing products of antenna functions with reduced matrix elements over all possible unresolved configurations. At NLO the subtraction term reads,

{\rm d}\sigma^{S}=\sum_{j}\,X_{ijk}^{0}\;|{\cal M}_{m}^{0}(1,\ldots,\tilde{I},\tilde{K},\ldots,m+1)|^{2}.

(7.10)

In Eq. (
7.10
),
X_{ijk}^{0}
is a tree-level three parton antenna, derived from a properly normalised physical matrix element that smoothly interpolates
the single soft and single collinear configurations. In the subtraction term, the particles
\tilde{I}
and
\tilde{K}
, form a colour connected hard antenna that radiated particle
j
. In doing so,
the momenta of the radiators change to form particles
i
and
k
. Depending on the flavour of the pair of hard radiators, the antennae can be quark-antiquark antennae, quark-gluon antennae,
or gluon-gluon antennae. As an example, the quark-antiquark antennae can be derived from the decay of a virtual photon into a quark-antiquark pair
\gamma^{*}\to q\bar{q}
+(partons). For the
quark-gluon-antiquark final state the corresponding antenna is:

A_{3}^{0}(1_{q},3_{g},2_{\bar{q}})=\frac{1}{s_{123}}\left(\frac{s_{13}}{s_{23}}+\frac{s_{23}}{s_{13}}+\frac{2s_{12}s_{123}}{s_{13}s_{23}}\right)+{\cal O}(\epsilon)\;,

(7.11)

which in the IR limits reproduces the universal soft and collinear singularities of tree-level QCD matrix elements,

\displaystyle A_{3}^{0}(1,3,2)

\displaystyle\mathrel{{\mathop{\longrightarrow}\limits}}

\displaystyle S_{132}\;,

(7.12)

\displaystyle A_{3}^{0}(1,3,2)

\displaystyle\mathrel{{\mathop{\longrightarrow}\limits}}

\displaystyle\frac{1}{s_{13}}P_{qg\to q}(z)\;,

(7.13)

\displaystyle A_{3}^{0}(1,3,2)

\displaystyle\mathrel{{\mathop{\longrightarrow}\limits}}

\displaystyle\frac{1}{s_{23}}P_{qg\to q}(z)\;.

(7.14)

A key ingredient in the evaluation of the subtraction term in equation (
7.10
) is the phase space mapping which relates the original momenta

p_{i},p_{j},p_{k}
describing the two hard radiator partons
i
and
k
and the emitted parton
j
to a redefined on-shell set
p_{\tilde{I}},p_{\tilde{K}}
which are linear combinations
of
p_{i},p_{j},p_{k}

[
183
,
184
]
22
22

22

Similar mappings aiming to have a local cancellation of IR divergencies
were studied for
fdu
in Section
3
for NLO.

\displaystyle p_{I}^{\mu}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle x\,p_{i}^{\mu}+r\,p_{j}^{\mu}+z\,p_{k}^{\mu}

\displaystyle p_{K}^{\mu}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(1-x)\,p_{i}^{\mu}+(1-r)\,p_{j}^{\mu}+(1-z)\,p_{k}^{\mu}\;

(7.15)

where,

\displaystyle x

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2(s_{ij}+s_{ik})}\Big[(1+\rho)\,s_{ijk}-2\,r\,s_{jk}\Big],

\displaystyle z

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2(s_{jk}+s_{ik})}\Big[(1-\rho)\,s_{ijk}-2\,r\,s_{ij}\Big],

\displaystyle\rho^{2}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 1+\frac{4\,r(1-r)\,s_{ij}s_{jk}}{s_{ijk}s_{ik}}.\;

(7.16)

The parameter
r
can be chosen conveniently
[
183
,
184
]
and we use
r=s_{jk}/(s_{ij}+s_{jk}).

The mapping (
7.15
) implements momentum conservation
p_{\tilde{I}}+p_{\tilde{K}}=p_{i}+p_{j}+p_{k}
and satisfies the following properties:

\displaystyle p_{\tilde{I}}^{2}=0,\qquad

\displaystyle\qquad p_{\tilde{K}}^{2}=0,

\displaystyle p_{\tilde{I}}\to p_{i},\qquad

\displaystyle\qquad p_{\tilde{K}}\to p_{k}\qquad\qquad\textrm{when {j} is soft},

\displaystyle p_{\tilde{I}}\to p_{i}+p_{j},\qquad

\displaystyle\qquad p_{\tilde{K}}\to p_{k}\qquad\qquad\textrm{when {i} becomes collinear with {j}},

\displaystyle p_{\tilde{I}}\to p_{i},\qquad

\displaystyle\qquad p_{\tilde{K}}\to p_{j}+p_{k}\qquad\textrm{when {j} becomes collinear with {k}}.

This guarantees the proper subtraction of infrared singularities. With this
mapping, the phase space factorises,

{\rm d\Phi}_{m+1}(p_{1},\ldots,p_{i},p_{j},p_{k},\ldots,p_{m+1})={\rm d\Phi}_{m}(p_{1},\ldots,p_{\tilde{I}},p_{\tilde{K}},\ldots,p_{m+1})\cdot{\rm d}\Phi_{X_{ijk}}(p_{i},p_{j},p_{k};p_{\tilde{I}}+p_{\tilde{K}})\\

(7.17)

such that the integration over the unresolved radiative degrees of freedom can be decoupled from the integration over the Born configurations. We then use (
7.17
) in (
7.10
) to obtain
the integrated counterpart of each of the subtraction terms, in a form that is suitable for the cancelation of the IR-singularities with the virtual contribution,

\displaystyle\int_{1}{\rm d}\sigma^{S}\;J_{m}^{(m)}(\{p_{m}\})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle|{\cal M}_{m}^{0}|^{2}\,J_{m}^{(m)}(\{p_{m}\})\;{\rm d}\Phi_{m}\int{\rm d}\Phi_{X_{ijk}}X_{ijk}^{0}

(7.18)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle|{\cal M}_{m}^{0}|^{2}\,J_{m}^{(m)}(\{p_{m}\})\;{\rm d}\Phi_{m}\;{\cal X}_{ijk}^{0}\;.

This integration is performed analytically in
d=4-2\epsilon
dimensions to make the infrared singularities
explicit, yielding the integrated three-parton antenna function
{\cal X}_{ijk}^{0}
. For the quark-gluon-antiquark final state the corresponding integrated antenna is,

\displaystyle{\cal A}_{3}^{0}(s_{123})=(s_{123})^{-\epsilon}\Bigg[\frac{1}{3\epsilon^{2}}+\frac{3}{2\epsilon}+\frac{19}{4}-\frac{7\pi^{2}}{12}+{\cal O}(\epsilon)\Bigg]=-2I_{q\bar{q}}^{(1)}(\epsilon,s_{123})+\frac{19}{4}\;,

(7.19)

where in the last equality the infrared singularity structure of the integrated antenna is written using the
{\mathbb{I}}^{(1)}
-operator
[
185
]
which
describes the singularity structure of virtual one-loop amplitudes. This results makes the cancellations between real and virtual corrections explicit using integrated antennae,
establishing the universality of the subtraction algorithm.

The extension of the scheme to the treatment of initial state radiation requires antennae with one or two radiators in the initial state (initial-final or initial-initial antennae). For those, the IR-singularity structure of
the integrated antennae contains the poles of the virtual one-loop contribution and simultaneously collinear poles originating from radiation off incoming partons
[
95
]
. The latter are cancelled by redefinition
(mass factorisation) of the parton distributions yielding a finite contribution, free of any poles in
\epsilon
that can be integrated numerically. The appropriate phase space factorisations and allowed
phase space mappings for these kinematical configurations are given in
[
95
]
.

7.2
Antenna subtraction at NNLO

At NNLO, there are three distinct
contributions due to double real radiation
{\rm d}\sigma_{NNLO}^{RR}
, mixed real-virtual radiation
{\rm d}\sigma_{NNLO}^{RV}

and double virtual radiation
{\rm d}\sigma_{NNLO}^{VV}
. The NNLO cross section becomes,

\displaystyle{\rm d}\sigma_{NNLO}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int_{{\rm d}\Phi_{m+2}}{\rm d}\sigma_{NNLO}^{RR}\;J_{m}^{(m+2)}(\{p_{m+2}\})

(7.20)

\displaystyle+

\displaystyle\int_{{\rm d}\Phi_{m+1}}{\rm d}\sigma_{NNLO}^{RV}\;J_{m}^{(m+1)}(\{p_{m+1}\})

\displaystyle+

\displaystyle\int_{{\rm d}\Phi_{m}}{\rm d}\sigma_{NNLO}^{VV}\;J_{m}^{(m)}(\{p_{m}\})\;.

For each matrix element the integration is over the appropriate phase space subject to the constraint that precisely
m
-jets are observed. As usual, the individual contributions in the
m
,
(m+1)
and
(m+2)
-parton final states are all separately infrared divergent. In the
(m+2)
-parton final state, two particles can become unresolved in several
possible configurations: double soft, soft/collinear, double single collinear, triple collinear. In each of these limits, the
(m+2)
-parton matrix element factorises into a reduced
m
-parton matrix element
times a generalised double unresolved factor. A detailed discussion of the kinematical definition of double unresolved limits is
available in
[
186
,
187
,
188
,
102
]
. In addition, in the
(m+1)
-parton final state, single unresolved soft and collinear
singularities arise in the real-virtual one-loop process.

As at NLO, one has to introduce subtraction terms for the
(m+1)
- and
(m+2)
-parton contributions. In this case we will explore
the factorised structure of QCD amplitudes at NNLO to derive the form of the antenna subtraction terms. Schematically, the NNLO
m
-jet cross section reads,

\displaystyle{\rm d}\sigma_{NNLO}

\displaystyle=\int_{{\rm d}\Phi_{m}}\Bigg({\rm d}\sigma_{NNLO}^{VV}\;J_{m}^{(m)}(\{p_{m}\})-{\rm d}\sigma_{NNLO}^{U}\;J_{m}^{(m)}(\{p_{m}\})\Bigg)

\displaystyle+

\displaystyle\int_{{\rm d}\Phi_{m+1}}\Bigg({\rm d}\sigma_{NNLO}^{RV}\;J_{m}^{(m+1)}(\{p_{m+1}\})-{\rm d}\sigma_{NNLO}^{T,1}\;J_{m}^{(m+1)}(\{p_{m+1}\})-{\rm d}\sigma_{NNLO}^{T,2}\;J_{m}^{(m)}(\{\tilde{p}_{m}\})\Bigg)

\displaystyle+

\displaystyle\int_{{\rm d}\Phi_{m+2}}\Bigg({\rm d}\sigma_{NNLO}^{RR}\;J_{m}^{(m+2)}(\{p_{m+2}\})-{\rm d}\sigma_{NNLO}^{S,1}\;J_{m}^{(m+1)}(\{\tilde{p}_{m+1}\})-{\rm d}\sigma_{NNLO}^{S,2}\;J_{m}^{(m)}(\{\tilde{p}_{m}\})\Bigg)\;.

By construction the last line is finite after the introduction of the subtraction terms for one-unresolved parton
{\rm d}\sigma_{NNLO}^{S,1}
and two unresolved partons
{\rm d}\sigma_{NNLO}^{S,2}
in
the double-real (
m+2
)-contribution. For the mixed real-virtual (
m+1
)-contribution, the explicit poles in the one-loop real-virtual matrix element cancel against the
integrated single unresolved real-radiation counterms as guaranteed by the KLN theorem, which are collected in
{\rm d}\sigma_{NNLO}^{T,1}
. The remaining counterterm
{\rm d}\sigma_{NNLO}^{T,2}

is by construction free of explicit
1/\epsilon
-poles and subtracts all phase space singularities of the physical real-virtual matrix-element and of the antenna subtracted
{\rm d}\sigma_{NNLO}^{T,1}
countertem.
Its contribution encodes the exact factorisation formula of one-loop matrix elements in the soft and collinear limits. Finally, the contribution
{\rm d}\sigma_{NNLO}^{U}
contains the integrated
counterparts of the antenna subtraction terms introduced at the double-real and real-virtual level and returns the explicit singularities of the double virtual matrix element. In this way, the three lines
in (
) are finite in
d=4
and can be safely evaluated with numerical methods.

7.2.1
Double-real contribution

In this section we establish the factorised form of all antenna subtraction terms for the double-real contribution. We begin by deriving the subtraction term for a single unresolved parton in the
double-real process. Since this configuration is NLO-type we can immediately use the result obtained in Section
7.1
and obtain,

{\rm d}\sigma^{S,1}={\rm d}\sigma^{S,a}=\sum_{j}\,X_{ijk}^{0}\;|{\cal M}_{m+1}^{0}(1,\ldots,\tilde{I},\tilde{K},\ldots,m+1)|^{2}.

(7.22)

With (
7.22
), singly unresolved limits involving parton-
j
in the antenna
X_{ijk}
cancel directly against the double-real matrix element.
However, contrary to the NLO case, the single-unresolved double-real subtraction term at NNLO factorises into an (
m+1
)-reduced matrix element and the jet function constrains
that precisely
m
-jets are observed. For this reason, singly unresolved limits as well as genuine double unresolved limits
involving the reduced matrix element in (
7.22
) are allowed and need to cancel with the genuine double-real doubly unresolved subtraction term
{\rm d}\sigma_{NNLO}^{S,2}
.

For the derivation of
{\rm d}\sigma_{NNLO}^{S,2}
we must distinguish the following configurations according to the colour connection of the double-unresolved partons:

(b)

Two colour-connected unresolved partons (colour-connected).

When two unresolved partons
j
and
k
are adjacent between radiators
i
and
l
the subtraction term is:

{{\rm d}\sigma^{S,b}}=\sum_{j}\,\Big(X_{ijkl}^{0}-X_{ijk}^{0}X_{IKl}^{0}-X_{jkl}^{0}X_{iJL}^{0}\Big)|{\cal M}_{m}^{0}(1,\ldots,\tilde{I},\tilde{L},\ldots,m)|^{2}\;,

(7.23)

where
X_{ijkl}^{0}
is a tree-level four parton antenna that smoothly interpolates all colour connected double unresolved limits. As an example, the final state quark-gluon-gluon-antiquark
antenna derived from
\gamma^{*}\to qgg\bar{q}
obeys the following factorisation properties,

\displaystyle A_{4}^{0}(q_{1},g_{3},g_{4},\bar{q}_{2})

\displaystyle\mathrel{{\mathop{\longrightarrow}\limits}}

\displaystyle S_{1342}\;,

(7.24)

\displaystyle A_{4}^{0}(q_{1},g_{3},g_{4},\bar{q}_{2})

\displaystyle\mathrel{{\mathop{\longrightarrow}\limits}}

\displaystyle P_{qgg\to Q}(x,y,z)\;,

(7.25)

\displaystyle A_{4}^{0}(q_{1},g_{3},g_{4},\bar{q}_{2})

\displaystyle\mathrel{{\mathop{\longrightarrow}\limits}}

\displaystyle S_{q;gg\bar{q}}P_{qg\to Q}(z)\;,

(7.26)

\displaystyle A_{4}^{0}(q_{1},g_{3},g_{4},\bar{q}_{2})

\displaystyle\mathrel{{\mathop{\longrightarrow}\limits}}

\displaystyle P_{qg\to Q}(z)P_{\bar{q}g\to Q}(y)\;,

(7.27)

where the universal double soft, triple collinear, soft/collinear and double single collinear limits listed above have been extensively discussed in the
literature
[
186
,
187
,
188
,
102
]
. The integrated counterpart of the four-parton antenna contribution exploits
the factorisation of the double-real radiation phase space,

\displaystyle{\rm d}\Phi_{m+2}(p_{1},\ldots,p_{m+2})={\rm d}\Phi_{m}(p_{1},\ldots,p_{\tilde{I}},p_{\tilde{L}},\ldots,p_{m+2})\cdot{\rm d}\Phi_{X_{ijkl}}(p_{i},p_{j},p_{k},p_{l})\;,

(7.28)

obtained by redefining a set of four massless on-shell momenta (radiator, two unresolved partons, radiator) into two on-shell massless momenta. The
mapping is defined as:
23
23

23

A preliminar proposal for mappings in
fdu
was provided in (
3.35
).
A detailed comparison between both approaches should be considered.
In fact,
fdu
could profit from the way how the various IR regions are split
in the antenna subtraction method.

\displaystyle p_{I}^{\mu}\equiv\widetilde{p_{(i,j,k)}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle x\,p_{i}+r_{1}\,p_{j}+r_{2}\,p_{k}+z\,p_{l},

\displaystyle p_{L}^{\mu}\equiv\widetilde{p_{(l,k,j)}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(1-x)\,p_{i}+(1-r_{1})\,p_{j}+(1-r_{2})\,p_{k}+(1-z)\,p_{l}\;,

(7.29)

with
p_{I}^{2}=p_{L}^{2}=0
.
Defining
s_{kl}=(p_{i_{k}}+p_{i_{l}})^{2}
,
the coefficients are given by
[
189
]
:

\displaystyle r_{1}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{s_{jk}+s_{jl}}{s_{ij}+s_{jk}+s_{jl}}

\displaystyle r_{2}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{s_{kl}}{s_{ik}+s_{jk}+s_{kl}}

\displaystyle x

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2(s_{ij}+s_{ik}+s_{il})}\Big[(1+\rho)\,s_{ijkl}-r_{1}\,(s_{jk}+2\,s_{jl})-r_{2}\,(s_{jk}+2\,s_{kl})

\displaystyle+(r_{1}-r_{2})\frac{s_{ij}s_{kl}-s_{ik}s_{jl}}{s_{il}}\Big]

\displaystyle z

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2(s_{il}+s_{jl}+s_{kl})}\Big[(1-\rho)\,s_{ijkl}-r_{1}\,(s_{jk}+2\,s_{ij})-r_{2}\,(s_{jk}+2\,s_{ik})

\displaystyle-(r_{1}-r_{2})\frac{s_{ij}s_{kl}-s_{ik}s_{jl}}{s_{il}}\Big]

\displaystyle\rho

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\Big[1+\frac{(r_{1}-r_{2})^{2}}{s_{il}^{2}\,s_{ijkl}^{2}}\,\lambda(s_{ij}\,s_{kl},s_{il}\,s_{jk},s_{ik}\,s_{jl})

\displaystyle+\frac{1}{s_{il}\,s_{ijkl}}\Big\{2\,\big(r_{1}\,(1-r_{2})+r_{2}(1-r_{1})\big)\big(s_{ij}s_{kl}+s_{ik}s_{jl}-s_{jk}s_{il}\big)

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad+\,4\,r_{1}\,(1-r_{1})\,s_{ij}s_{jl}+4\,r_{2}\,(1-r_{2})\,s_{ik}s_{kl}\Big\}\Big]^{\frac{1}{2}}\;,

\displaystyle\lambda(u,v,w)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle u^{2}+v^{2}+w^{2}-2(uv+uw+vw)\;.

This mapping smoothly interpolates all colour connected double unresolved singularities. It satisfies the following properties:

\displaystyle\widetilde{p_{(ijk)}}\to p_{i},

\displaystyle\widetilde{p_{(lkj)}}\to p_{l}\hskip 71.13188pt

when
j,k\to 0
,

\displaystyle\widetilde{p_{(ijk)}}\to p_{i}+p_{j}+p_{k},

\displaystyle\widetilde{p_{(lkj)}}\to p_{l}\hskip 71.13188pt

when
i//j//k
,

\displaystyle\widetilde{p_{(ijk)}}\to p_{i},

\displaystyle\widetilde{p_{(lkj)}}\to p_{l}+p_{k}+p_{j}\hskip 17.07182pt

when
j//k//l
,

\displaystyle\widetilde{p_{(ijk)}}\to p_{i},

\displaystyle\widetilde{p_{(lkj)}}\to p_{l}+p_{k}\hskip 42.67912pt

when
j\to 0+k//l
,

\displaystyle\widetilde{p_{(ijk)}}\to p_{i}+p_{j},

\displaystyle\widetilde{p_{(lkj)}}\to p_{j}\hskip 68.28644pt

when
k\to 0+i//j
,

\displaystyle\widetilde{p_{(ijk)}}\to p_{i}+p_{j},

\displaystyle\widetilde{p_{(lkj)}}\to p_{k}+p_{l}\hskip 42.67912pt

when
i//j+k//l
.

which guarantee that all double unresolved colour connected IR-singularities are properly subtracted. Moreover, in single unresolved limits, the momentum mapping above collapses into an
NLO mapping (
7.15
), thereby allowing the products of three-parton antenna functions in (
7.23
) to subtract the single unresolved limits of the associated four parton antenna.

(c)

Two unresolved partons that are not colour connected but share a common
radiator (almost colour-unconnected).

There are double unresolved configurations where the unresolved partons are separated by a hard radiator parton, for example,
i,j,k,l,m
where
j
and
l
are unresolved. In this case
we take the strongly ordered approach where
i,j,k
form an antenna with hard partons
I
and
K
yielding an ordered amplitude involving
I,K,l,m
. The case where
l
is
unresolved is then treated using an antenna
K,l,m
with hard partons
K^{\prime}
and
M^{\prime}
. The other case where first
k,l,m
form an antenna followed by
i,j,K
is also included where the
momenta are obtained by iterative use of the NLO momentum mappings. The subtraction term is,

\displaystyle{\rm d}\sigma_{NNLO}^{S,c}=

\displaystyle-

\displaystyle\sum_{j,l}X_{ijk}^{0}X_{Klm}^{0}|{\cal M}^{0}_{m}(p_{1},\ldots,p_{I},p_{K^{\prime}},p_{M^{\prime}},\ldots,p_{m})|^{2}

(7.30)

\displaystyle-

\displaystyle\sum_{j,l}X_{mlk}^{0}X_{ijK}^{0}|{\cal M}^{0}_{m}(p_{1},\ldots,p_{I^{\prime}},p_{K^{\prime}},p_{M},\ldots,p_{m})|^{2}\;.

(d)

Two unresolved partons that are well separated from each other
in the colour
chain (colour-unconnected).

When two unresolved partons
j
and
o
are completely disconnected i.e. for colour ordered amplitudes of the type
{\cal M}(...,i,j,k,...,n,o,p,...)
, the double-real matrix element
factorises into the product of two uncorrelated single unresolved factors with hard partons
I,K
and
N,P
respectively. The subtraction term is,

\displaystyle{\rm d}\sigma_{NNLO}^{S,d}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\sum_{j,o}X_{ijk}^{0}X_{nop}^{0}|{\cal M}^{0}_{m}(p_{1},\ldots,p_{I},p_{K},\ldots,p_{N},p_{P},\ldots,p_{m})|^{2}\;.

(7.31)

(e)

Large angle soft emission.

By taking a strongly ordered subtraction of the unresolved limits, an uncanceled contribution involving the inner and outer antennae in the iterated subtracted structures defined above,
leads to an incomplete subtraction of large-angle soft gluon radiation. To account for the left over single soft-gluon emission contribution at large angles, an additional subtraction term is defined,

\displaystyle{\rm d}\sigma_{NNLO}^{S,e}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{j}X_{IlK}^{0}\Big(S_{I^{\prime}jK^{\prime}}-S_{IjK}-S_{ajI^{\prime}}+S_{ajI}-S_{K^{\prime}jb}+S_{Kjb}\Big)

(7.32)

\displaystyle\times

\displaystyle|{\cal M}^{0}_{m}(p_{1},\ldots,p_{a},p_{I^{\prime}},p_{K^{\prime}},p_{b},\ldots,p_{m})|^{2}\;.

The large-angle soft subtraction term contains soft antenna functions of the form
S_{ajb}
which are simply the eikonal factor for a soft gluon
j
emitted between hard partons
a
and
b
. The
soft factors are associated with an NLO antenna phase space mapping
(i,j,k)\to(I,K)
, followed by a second NLO antenna phase space mapping
(I,l,K)\to(I^{\prime},K^{\prime})
.

Subtraction of angular correlations

When using the antenna subtraction method to construct subtraction terms for higher order calculations, one encounters the problem of angular correlations in the collinear splitting of a gluon into massless partons. These
angular correlations introduce non-factorizing terms which correlate the hard reduced matrix element with the splitting functions. As an example, for the
gg\to g
splitting, the purely gluonic four
parton antenna function factorises into the corresponding tensorial splitting functions and tensorial three parton antenna functions,

\displaystyle F_{4}^{0}(g_{1},g_{2},g_{3},g_{4})

\displaystyle\mathrel{{\mathop{\longrightarrow}\limits}}

\displaystyle\frac{1}{s_{ij}}P_{gg\to G}^{\mu\nu}(z)(F_{3}^{0})_{\mu\nu}((ij),k,l)

(7.33)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{s_{ij}}P_{gg\to G}(z)F_{3}^{0}((ij),k,l)+{\rm ang.}

P_{ij\to(ij)}^{\mu\nu}
stands for the spin dependent gluon splitting function given
by
[
58
]
,

\displaystyle P_{gg}^{\mu\nu}=2\left[-g^{\mu\nu}\left(\frac{z}{1-z}+\frac{1-z}{z}\right)-2(1-\epsilon)z(1-z)\frac{k_{\perp}^{\mu}k_{\perp}^{\nu}}{k_{\perp}^{2}}\right],

(7.34)

while
P_{ij\to(ij)}
stands for the spin averaged gluon splitting function:

P_{gg\to g}(z)=2\left(\frac{z}{1-z}+\frac{1-z}{z}+z(1-z)\right).

(7.35)

The tensorial structure of the three-parton antenna function
(F_{3}^{0})_{\mu\nu}
is obtained by leaving the polarisation index of the gluon associated with momentum
P^{\mu}
uncontracted and can be derived by analogy with the scalar three-parton antenna functions from physical matrix elements.

Since we use spin-averaged scalar antenna functions to remove unresolved limits in QCD amplitudes, these do not subtract angular correlations in gluon-splittings. However, these angular terms vanish when the azimuthal
variable of the collinear system is integrated out. This can be seen for the single collinear limits using the standard
momentum parametrisation
[
58
,
190
]
for the
i_{g}\parallel j_{g}
limit:

\displaystyle p_{i}^{\mu}=zp^{\mu}+k_{\perp}^{\mu}-\frac{k_{\perp}^{2}}{z}\frac{n^{\mu}}{2p\cdot n}\;,

\displaystyle p_{j}^{\mu}=(1-z)p^{\mu}-k_{\perp}^{\mu}-\frac{k_{\perp}^{2}}{1-z}\frac{n^{\mu}}{2p\cdot n}\;,

\displaystyle\mbox{with }2p_{i}\cdot p_{j}=-\frac{k_{\perp}^{2}}{z(1-z)}\;,

\displaystyle p^{2}=n^{2}=k_{\perp}.p=k_{\perp}.n=0\;.

(7.36)

Here
p^{\mu}
denotes the collinear momentum direction, and
n^{\mu}
is an
auxiliary vector. The collinear limit is approached as
k_{\perp}^{2}\to 0
.

In the simple collinear
i\parallel j
limit of the four-parton antenna function

F_{4}^{0}(l_{g},i_{g},j_{g},k_{g})
,
one chooses
n=p_{k}
to be one of the non-collinear momenta, such that the
antenna function can be expressed in terms of
p
,
n
,
k_{\perp}
and
p_{l}
.
Expanding in
k_{\perp}^{\mu}
yields only non-vanishing scalar products of the
form
p_{l}\cdot k_{\perp}
. Expressing the integral over the antenna phase
space in the
(p,n)
centre-of-mass frame, the angular average can be
carried out as

\frac{1}{2\pi}\int_{0}^{2\pi}{\rm d}\phi\,(p_{l}\cdot k_{\perp})=0\;,\qquad\frac{1}{2\pi}\int_{0}^{2\pi}{\rm d}\phi\,(p_{l}\cdot k_{\perp})^{2}=-k_{\perp}^{2}\,\frac{p\cdot p_{l}\,n\cdot p_{l}}{p\cdot n}\;.

(7.37)

In this frame, the unsubtracted angular correlation in the gluon-gluon collinear limit of the four-parton purely gluonic antenna function is given by,

\displaystyle\Theta_{F_{3}^{0}}(i,j,z,k_{\perp})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left[\frac{1}{s_{ij}}P^{\mu\nu}_{ij\to(ij)}(z,k_{\perp})(F_{3}^{0})_{\mu\nu}-\frac{1}{s_{ij}}P_{ij\to(ij)}(z)F_{3}^{0}(1,(ij),2)\right]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{4}{s_{ij}^{2}s_{1p2}^{2}}\left(\frac{s_{12}^{2}s_{1p2}^{2}+s_{1p}^{2}s_{p2}^{2}}{s_{12}^{2}s_{1p}^{2}s_{p2}^{2}}\right)\Bigg[s_{12}s_{1p}s_{p2}\;k_{\perp}\cdot k_{\perp}
